=== Chlorpheniramine/Codeine (Merck Manual Professional) ===

NURSING QUICK-REFERENCE SUMMARY
Auto-generated on 2026-09-25 from the prescribing information in this file; full details follow below.

Drug Class: Chlorpheniramine; codeine is an antihistamine-antitussive combination product.

Routes: oral; Common adult doses: 20 mL/day PO of extended-release (ER) oral suspension or 2 tablets/day PO of ER tablets.

Common Side Effects (excluding routine GI upset): cyanosis, dyspnea, increased intracranial pressure, pulmonary edema, respiratory arrest, respiratory depression, agitation, confusion, depression, dizziness, drowsiness, dyskinesia, euphoria, excitability, headache, impaired cognition, insomnia, irritability, restlessness, seizures, tremor, gastroesophageal reflux, ileus, opioid-induced esophageal dysfunction, biliary obstruction, hyperamylasemia, pancreatitis, bradycardia, hypertension, hypotension

In an Overdose: Severe overdose can cause mydriasis once brain anoxia develops. In children less than 12 years, these drug products have been associated with fatal respiratory depression; postmarketing events seen in children less than 12 years of age include accidental overdose, bronchopneumonia, coma, cyanosis...

Key Nursing Considerations: Contraindications/precautions: NOTE: This monograph discusses the use of a chlorpheniramine and codeine combination for the temporary relief of cough and associated allergies.

Indication Specific Dosing
  General Information
  When discontinuing chlorpheniramine; codeine therapy in individuals who are opioid-dependent, taper the dose gradually by 25% to 50% every 2 to 4 days. If the individual develops signs and symptoms of withdrawal, raise the dose to the previous level and taper more slowly by increasing the interval between doses, decreasing the amount of change in dose, or both.
  Indications
    Routes
      Heading
      Oral dosage (extended-release oral suspension, e.g., Tuzistra XR,- codeine polistirex/chlorpheniramine polistirex providing 14.7 mg of codeine with 2.8 mg of chlorpheniramine per 5 mL)
      Route Age Groups
        Heading
        Adults
        Dosage Description
        10 mL PO every 12 hours. Max: Do not exceed 20 mL total in 24 hours. Lower doses may be more appropriate for geriatric or debilitated patients, dependent upon renal and hepatic function, and/or concomitant medications. LIMITATION OF USE: Reserve chlorpheniramine; codeine for use in adults for whom alternative treatment options are ineffective, not tolerated, or would be otherwise inadequate to provide sufficient management of cough. Prescribe for the shortest duration consistent with individual patient treatment goals. Monitor patients closely for respiratory depression, especially within the first 24 to 72 hours of initiating therapy; proper dosing is essential to reduce the risk of respiratory depression. Reevaluate patients with unresponsive cough in 5 days or sooner for possible underlying pathology, such as foreign body or lower respiratory tract disease. CONTINUED TREATMENT: If a patient requires a refill, reevaluate the cause of the cough and the need for continued treatment within 5 days. DISCONTINUATION: Do not rapidly reduce the dose or abruptly discontinue this product in a physically-dependent patient; taper the dose gradually, by 25% to 50% every 2 to 4 days, while monitoring carefully for signs and symptoms of withdrawal. If withdrawal occurs, raise the dose to the previous level and taper more slowly, either by increasing the interval between decreases, decreasing the amount of change in dose, or both.
      Heading
      Oral dosage (extended-release tablets of codeine phosphate and chlorpheniramine maleate, e.g., Tuxarin ER)
      Route Age Groups
        Heading
        Adults
        Dosage Description
        1 extended-release (ER) tablet PO every 12 hours. Each ER tablet contains 54.3 mg of codeine phosphate (equivalent to 40 mg of codeine) with 8 mg of chlorpheniramine maleate (equivalent to 5.6 mg of chlorpheniramine). Max: Do not exceed 2 tablets per 24 hours. Lower doses may be more appropriate for geriatric or debilitated patients, dependent upon renal and hepatic function, and/or concomitant medications. LIMITATION OF USE: Reserve chlorpheniramine; codeine for use in adults for whom alternative treatment options are ineffective, not tolerated, or would be otherwise inadequate to provide sufficient management of cough. Prescribe for the shortest duration consistent with individual patient treatment goals. Monitor patients closely for respiratory depression, especially within the first 24 to 72 hours of initiating therapy; proper dosing is essential to reduce the risk of respiratory depression. Reevaluate unresponsive cough in 5 days or sooner for possible underlying pathology, such as foreign body or lower respiratory tract disease. CONTINUED TREATMENT: If a patient requires a refill, reevaluate the cause of the cough and the need for continued treatment within 5 days. DISCONTINUATION: Do not rapidly reduce the dose or abruptly discontinue this product in a physically-dependent patient; taper the dose gradually, by 25% to 50% every 2 to 4 days, while monitoring carefully for signs and symptoms of withdrawal. If withdrawal occurs, raise the dose to the previous level and taper more slowly, either by increasing the interval between decreases, decreasing the amount of change in dose, or both.
    Heading
    For the temporary relief of cough and upper respiratory symptoms (e.g., rhinorrhea, sneezing, itching of the nose or throat or itchy, watery eyes) associated with allergy (allergic rhinitis) or the common cold

Contraindications And Precautions
  Headline
  Contraindications/Warnings/Precautions
  General Information
  NOTE: This monograph discusses the use of a chlorpheniramine and codeine combination for the temporary relief of cough and associated allergies. Clinicians may wish to consult the individual monographs for more information about each agent.
 
Although opiate hypersensitivity is rare, patients who have demonstrated a prior codeine hypersensitivity reaction should not receive products containing codeine. Patients with an opiate agonist hypersensitivity to the phenanthrene subclass including oxycodone and morphine should also avoid codeine administration since cross-sensitivity may occur. Similarly, those with a known chlorpheniramine hypersensitivity should not receive products containing chlorpheniramine.
 
Antitussives should be avoided if possible when a productive cough is present, such as may occur with asthma, emphysema, chronic bronchitis, or tobacco smoking. Chlorpheniramine; codeine should be avoided during an acute asthmatic attack (status asthmaticus). The anticholinergic activity of H1-antagonists may result in thickened bronchial secretions and aggravate an acute asthmatic attack; opiate agonists may decrease respiratory reserve. Chlorpheniramine; codeine should also be avoided in those with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD), upper airway obstruction, and patients with respiratory insufficiency or significant respiratory depression since respiratory status may be further compromised. The use of chlorpheniramine; codeine may be appropriate in some patients with respiratory conditions, especially if thickened bronchial secretions are not present. Benefit versus risk should be assessed in pediatric patients with respiratory difficulties such as may be seen in cases of croup.
 
Similar to other opiate agonists, the use of codeine should be avoided during the postoperative period of surgery if possible, since the drug suppresses the cough reflex.
 
Although chlorpheniramine has only moderate anticholinergic effects, a worsening of symptoms may be seen in patients with bladder obstruction, benign prostatic hypertrophy, or urinary retention. Codeine and other opiate agonists can cause urinary retention and oliguria, due to increased detrusor muscle tension, also resulting in exacerbation of the above conditions, as well as urethral stricture, or pelvic tumors. Due to the effects of both chlorpheniramine and codeine on the gastrointestinal tract, this combination should be used cautiously in patients with GI disease including GI obstruction or ileus, irritable bowel syndrome, ulcerative colitis, peptic ulcer disease, or constipation. Opiate agonists may obscure the diagnosis or clinical course in patients with acute abdominal conditions. Patients with acute ulcerative colitis (UC) or other inflammatory bowel disease may be more sensitive to the constipating effects of opiate agonists. Drug-induced obstructive bowel disease may result from chronic administration of an opiate. Codeine should be avoided if possible in patients with diarrhea secondary to poisoning or infection, until the toxic substance has been eliminated.
 
Both chlorpheniramine and codeine can cause drowsiness due to CNS depression. Additive sedative effects are possible. Patients receiving chlorpheniramine; codeine should be advised to avoid driving or operating machinery until the effects of the drug are known. Additive drowsiness may be seen when used with other CNS depressant agents including ethanol. Chlorpheniramine; codeine should be used cautiously in those with alcoholism or ethanol intoxication.
 
Chlorpheniramine; codeine should be used cautiously in debilitated patients or the elderly. Increased susceptibility to the anticholinergic effects of the drug is possible since there is a decline in endogenous cholinergic activity that occurs with age. In addition, elderly patients are more susceptible to adverse reactions from opiate agonists, especially sedation and respiratory depression, possibly as a result of altered distribution of the drug and decreased elimination. Although specific guidelines are not available, initial doses may need to be reduced.
 
Chlorpheniramine; codeine should not be used in patients with closed-angle glaucoma. Increased intraocular pressure may occur from the anticholinergic actions of chlorpheniramine, precipitating an acute attack of glaucoma. Elderly patients are more susceptible to these effects, including possible precipitation of undiagnosed glaucoma. Other ocular effects resulting from the anticholinergic effects of chlorpheniramine include dry eyes or blurred vision. Ocular effects may be of significance in the elderly and those who wear contact lenses.
 
Drug accumulation or prolonged duration of action is possible in patients with renal impairment or renal failure. In acute situations, patients require close monitoring to avoid excessive toxicity. Patients with chronic renal disease may require lower dosages of chlorpheniramine; codeine.
 
The quinidine-like local anesthetic and anticholinergic effects of H1-antagonists are responsible for adverse cardiac effects that have been observed with such drugs, including tachycardia, ECG changes, hypotension, and arrhythmias. Codeine may produce cholinergic side effects by stimulating medullary vagal nuclei causing bradycardia, vasovagal syncope, and histamine release, with subsequent peripheral vasodilation and orthostatic hypotension. Although these cardiovascular effects are uncommon, chlorpheniramine; codeine should be used conservatively in patients with cardiac disease, including those with angina, cardiac arrhythmias, cardiomyopathy, heart failure, hypertension, hypotension, hypovolemia, or other cardiac conditions. Codeine should be used cautiously in patients with adrenal insufficiency (e.g., Addison's disease or chronic exogenous supplementation), hypothyroidism, or myxedema due to the hypotensive effects of the drug.
 
Chlorpheniramine; codeine is extensively metabolized in the liver and metabolism may be reduced in the presence of hepatic impairment. Those with significant hepatic disease receiving chlorpheniramine; codeine should be monitored for liver function and side effects. Dosage adjustments may be required as drug accumulation or prolonged duration of action can occur in patients with hepatic dysfunction.
 
Seizures can be precipitated by opiate agonists in patients with a preexisting seizure disorder. The incidence of these effects during codeine therapy is not known, but appears to be rare at normal doses.
 
Use of chlorpheniramine; codeine in children less than 6 years of age or infants is not recommended. Antihistamines may induce paradoxical CNS stimulation in some children. Similarly, the use of codeine in premature infants and children < 2 years of age may be associated with respiratory arrest, coma, and death due to an increased susceptibility to the respiratory depressant action of the drug. Chlorpheniramine; codeine should not be used in neonates due to the possibility of chlorpheniramine-induced paradoxical CNS stimulation or seizures. In January 2007, the CDC warned caregivers and healthcare providers of the risk for serious injury or fatal overdose from the administration of cough and cold products to children and infants less than 2 years of age. This warning followed an investigation of the deaths of three (3) infants less than 6 months of age that were attributed to the inadvertent inappropriate use of these products. The symptoms preceding these deaths have not been clearly defined, and there is a lack of conclusive data describing the exact cause of death. The report estimated that 1519 children less than 2 years of age were treated in emergency departments during 2004—2005 for adverse events related to cough and cold medications. In October 2007, the FDA Nonprescription Drug Advisory Committee and the Pediatric Advisory Committee recommended that nonprescription cough and cold products containing pseudoephedrine, dextromethorphan, chlorpheniramine, diphenhydramine, brompheniramine, phenylephrine, clemastine, or guaifenesin not be used in children less than 6 years of age. In January 2008, the FDA issued a Public Health Advisory recommending that OTC cough and cold products not be used in infants and children less than 2 years. An official ruling regarding the use of these products in children greater than 2 years has not yet been announced. The FDA recommends that if parents and caregivers use cough and cold products in children greater than 2 years, labels should be read carefully, caution should be used when administering multiple products, and only measuring devices specifically designed for use with medications should be used. While some combination cough/cold products containing these ingredients are available by prescription only and are not necessarily under scrutiny by the FDA, clinicians should thoroughly assess each patient's use of similar products, both prescription and nonprescription, to avoid duplication of therapy and the potential for inadvertent overdose.
 
Chlorpheniramine; codeine is classified as FDA pregnancy risk category C. Both codeine and chlorpheniramine cross the placenta. In general, H1-antagonists are not recommended for use in the last 2 weeks of pregnancy due to a possible association between these drugs and retrolental fibroplasia in premature neonates. No well controlled trials of chlorpheniramine; codeine use in pregnant women are available. One retrospective study found that the children of women who received chlorpheniramine during pregnancy had a statistically significant increased incidence of inguinal hernia and eye or ear abnormalities. Codeine-induced respiratory depression may occur in the fetus when codeine is administered to the expectant mother shortly prior to or during labor. Routine use of opiate agonists by an expectant mother can lead to depressed respiration in the newborn and a neonatal abstinence syndrome. Withdrawal symptoms in a newborn occur 1—4 days after birth and include generalized tremors, hypertonicity with any form of tactile stimuli, hyperalterness, sleeplessness, excessive crying, vomiting, diarrhea, yawning and fever. Codeine, as a single entity, increases to FDA category D if used for prolonged periods or in high doses close to term. Chlorpheniramine; codeine should be administered during pregnancy only when the benefits of therapy outweigh the potential risks to the fetus. Non-pharmacologic methods (e.g., fluids, and rest) are the preferred treatments for symptomatic relief of colds or allergies during pregnancy.
 
Chlorpheniramine; codeine should be avoided if possible during breast-feeding since both agents are excreted into breast milk. Chlorpheniramine may produce side effects in breast-feeding infants such as irritability, disturbed sleeping patterns, drowsiness, hyperexcitability, or excessive crying. Administration of opiates to breast-feeding women may result in CNS and respiratory depressant effects in the infant. Alternatively, withdrawal symptoms may occur in nursing infants whose mothers discontinue chronic opioid therapy. Other methods of feeding should be used if routine use of chlorpheniramine; codeine therapy is necessary in the breast-feeding mother.
 
Opiates can increase cerebrospinal fluid pressure and produce cerebral hypoxia through their respiratory depressant effects, which may result in exacerbation of a head trauma, or other conditions in which increased intracranial pressure is present. Furthermore, administration of codeine may compromise the evaluation of neurologic parameters in patients with such conditions; therefore, the drug should not be given unless the benefits outweigh the risk.
 
Abrupt discontinuation of prolonged codeine therapy can result in withdrawal symptoms, and the drug should be gradually tapered. Similarly, abrupt discontinuation after chronic use of agents with anticholinergic properties, such as chlorpheniramine, may result in rebound cholinergic effects, such as insomnia, nausea, and diarrhea.
 
Because codeine is an opiate agonist, it is subject to substance abuse and psychologic dependence (i.e., drug addiction) or criminal diversion. Drug addiction is characterized by compulsive use, use for non-medical purposes, and continued use despite harm or risk for harm. Patients with a previous history of substance abuse may be at increased risk of relapse if treated with codeine. Abuse and addiction are separate and distinct from physiologic dependence and tolerance. Physicians should be aware that psychologic dependence may not be accompanied by concurrent tolerance and symptoms of physiologic dependence. In addition, abuse of opiate agonists can occur in the absence of true psychologic dependence and is characterized by misuse for non-medical purposes, often in combination with other psychoactive substances.
 
Morphine is well recognized to increase the tone of the biliary tract causing spasms (especially in the sphincter of Oddi) and to increase biliary tract pressure. Biliary effects of opiate agonists may result in plasma amylase and lipase concentrations up to 2—15 times the normal values. The clinical significance of these effects during codeine therapy specifically is not known. Nevertheless, codeine should be used with caution in patients with biliary tract disease or undergoing biliary tract surgery as codeine is metabolized to morphine.
  Contraindications And Precautions
    Headline
    Drug Interactions
    General Information
    The coadministration of certain medications may lead to harm and require avoidance or therapy modification; review all drug interactions prior to concomitant use of other medications.
    Headline
    Hypersensitivity
    General Information
    This medication is contraindicated in patients with a history of hypersensitivity to it or any of its components. Chlorpheniramine is a first-generation antihistamine of the alkylamine class. Allergies to one antihistamine can sometimes lead to cross-reactivity with other chemically-related antihistamines.
    Headline
    major psychiatric disorder, opioid overdose, risk of medication errors, substance abuse disorder
    General Information
    Opioids expose users to the risks of addiction, abuse, and misuse. Although the risk of addiction in any individual is unknown, it can occur in individuals appropriately prescribed an opioid. Addiction can occur at recommended dosages and if the medication is misused or abused. The risk of opioid-related overdose or overdose-related death is increased with higher doses, and this risk persists over the course of therapy. The potential for these risks should not prevent the proper management of pain in any given individual. Assess an individual's risk for opioid addiction, abuse, or misuse before prescribing an opioid, and monitor for the development of these behaviors or conditions. Risks are increased in individuals with a personal or family history of substance abuse disorder (including alcohol or drug abuse or addiction) or major psychiatric disorder (e.g., major depression). Individuals at increased risk may be prescribed opioids, but use in such individuals necessitates intensive counseling about the risks and proper use of the opioid along with frequent reevaluation for signs of addiction, abuse, and misuse. Opioids are sought for nonmedical use and are subject to diversion from legitimate prescribed use. Consider these risks when prescribing or dispensing opioids. Strategies to reduce these risks include prescribing the medication in the smallest appropriate quantity and educating about proper medication storage and disposal. Keep opioids out of the reach of children, others for whom the medication was not prescribed, and pets as accidental exposure or improper use may cause respiratory failure and a fatal overdose. Accidental exposure of even a single dose of an opioid, especially by younger individuals, can result in a fatal overdose. To reduce the risk of medication errors, ensure that dosing is communicated clearly and dispensed accurately. Discuss the importance of an opioid overdose reversal agent and the options for obtaining an opioid overdose reversal agent (e.g., prescription, over-the-counter, or as part of a community-based program), especially with individuals who are at increased risk of opioid overdose, such as those who are also using other CNS depressants, who have a history of opioid use disorder (OUD), who have experienced a previous opioid overdose, or who have household members or other close contacts at risk for accidental exposure or opioid overdose. Educate individuals and caregivers on how to recognize respiratory depression, and how to use an opioid overdose reversal agent for the emergency treatment of opioid overdose. Emphasize the importance of calling 911 or getting emergency medical help, even if an opioid overdose reversal agent is administered.
    Headline
    asthma exacerbation, cachexia, central sleep apnea, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, coadministration with certain medications, cor pulmonale, hypercapnia, hypoxia, respiratory depression, respiratory insufficiency, severe asthma
    General Information
    Codeine is contraindicated in individuals with significant respiratory depression and those with asthma exacerbation or severe asthma in an unmonitored setting or in the absence of resuscitative equipment. Avoid opioid coadministration with certain medications, specifically other CNS depressants when possible, as this significantly increases the risk for profound sedation, respiratory depression, coma, and death. Reserve concomitant prescribing of these medications for use in individuals for whom alternative treatment options are inadequate; if concurrent use is necessary, use the lowest effective dosages and minimum treatment durations needed. Monitor individuals closely for signs or symptoms of respiratory depression and sedation. Consider prescribing an opioid overdose reversal agent if concomitant use is warranted. Careful monitoring is required, particularly when CYP2D6 inhibitors or CYP3A4 inhibitors or inducers are used concomitantly with codeine. Concurrent use of a CYP3A4 inhibitor or discontinuation of a concurrently used CYP3A4 inducer may result in an increase in plasma codeine concentrations with subsequent greater metabolism by CYP2D6, resulting in greater morphine concentrations, which may increase the risk for respiratory depression, or discontinuation of a concomitantly used CYP2D6 inhibitor may result in a decrease in plasma codeine concentrations and an increase in plasma morphine concentrations, which may increase the risk for respiratory depression. Concurrent use of a CYP3A4 inducer or discontinuation of a CYP3A4 inhibitor may result in lower codeine concentrations, greater norcodeine concentrations, and less metabolism by CYP2D6 with resultant lower morphine concentrations, which may be associated with reduced efficacy and/or opioid withdrawal. Similarly, concurrent use of a CYP2D6 inhibitor may result in an increase in plasma codeine concentrations and a decrease in plasma morphine concentrations, which may be associated with reduced efficacy and/or opioid withdrawal. Individuals with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD), cor pulmonale, respiratory insufficiency, hypoxia, hypercapnia, or preexisting respiratory depression are at increased risk of decreased respiratory drive even at recommended doses. Individuals with advanced age, cachexia, or debilitation are also at an increased risk for opioid-induced respiratory depression. Monitor such individuals closely, particularly when initiating and titrating the opioid; consider the use of non-opioid analgesics in these individuals. Carbon dioxide retention from opioid-induced respiratory depression may also worsen opioid sedating effects. Consider decreasing the opioid dosage in individuals with central sleep apnea (CSA). Opioids increase the risk of CSA and sleep-related hypoxemia in a dose-dependent fashion.
    Headline
    brain tumor, coma, head trauma, increased intracranial pressure, loss of consciousness
    General Information
    Avoid opioid use in individuals with loss of consciousness or coma; opioids may obscure the clinical course in an individual with a head trauma injury. Monitor individuals who may be susceptible to the intracranial effect of carbon dioxide retention (e.g., those with evidence of increased intracranial pressure or brain tumor) for signs of sedation and respiratory depression, particularly when initiating opioid therapy. Opioids may reduce respiratory drive and the resultant carbon dioxide retention can further increase intracranial pressure.
    Headline
    CYP2D6 ultrarapid metabolizers
    General Information
    Do not use codeine in individuals who are CYP2D6 ultrarapid metabolizers. Some individuals may be ultrarapid metabolizers due to a specific CYP2D6 genotype (gene duplications noted as *1/*1xN or *1/*2xN). Ultrarapid metabolizers convert codeine into morphine more rapidly and completely than other people. Higher than expected serum morphine concentrations occur due to the rapid conversion and serious toxicity including life-threatening or fatal respiratory depression may occur. The prevalence of this CYP2D6 phenotype varies widely and has been estimated at 1% to 10% in Whites, 3% to 4% in Blacks, 1% to 2% in East Asians (Chinese, Japanese, Korean), and may be greater than 10% in certain racial/ethnic groups (e.g., Oceanian, Northern African, Middle Eastern, Ashkenazi Jews, Puerto Rican). Approximately 7% to 10% of the White population lacks functional CYP2D6 activity.
    Headline
    activities requiring coordination and concentration, driving or operating machinery
    General Information
    Advise individuals against driving or operating machinery or participating in activities requiring coordination and concentration during opioid therapy unless they are tolerant to the effects of opioids and know how they will react to the medication. Opioids can impair the mental or physical abilities needed to perform potentially hazardous activities.
    Headline
    hypovolemia, shock
    General Information
    Avoid the use of opioids in individuals with circulatory shock. Opioids may cause vasodilation that can further reduce cardiac output and blood pressure. Monitor individuals whose ability to maintain blood pressure has been compromised by hypovolemia or concurrent administration of certain CNS depressant medications (e.g., phenothiazines, general anesthetics) for hypotension after opioid treatment initiation or dosage titration due to increased risk for severe hypotension.
    Headline
    acute pancreatitis, biliary tract disease, GI obstruction
    General Information
    Chlorpheniramine; codeine is contraindicated in individuals with known or suspected GI obstruction, including paralytic ileus. Opioids may cause spasm of the sphincter of Oddi or increases in serum amylase. Monitor individuals with biliary tract disease, including acute pancreatitis, for worsening symptoms.
    Headline
    hepatic failure
    General Information
    Use a lower initial dose of codeine or a longer dosing interval in individuals with hepatic impairment or hepatic failure and titrate the dose slowly. Monitor for adverse events such as respiratory depression, sedation, and hypotension. No formal studies have been conducted with codeine in individuals with hepatic impairment, and the pharmacokinetics of codeine in this population are unknown.
    Headline
    renal failure
    General Information
    Chlorpheniramine; codeine should be used with caution in individuals with severe renal impairment or renal failure, and these individuals should be monitored closely for codeine effects such as respiratory depression, sedation, and hypotension. Both chlorpheniramine and codeine accumulation or prolonged duration of action may occur in individuals with severe renal impairment. In acute situations, such individuals require close monitoring to avoid excessive toxicity. Individuals with chronic renal disease may require lower doses or less frequent dosing intervals.
    Headline
    prostatic hypertrophy
    General Information
    Due to the anticholinergic effects which may aggravate urinary retention, first-generation antihistamines, such as chlorpheniramine, should be used with caution in people with trouble urinating due to prostatic hypertrophy.
    Headline
    glaucoma
    General Information
    Chlorpheniramine should be used with caution in people with glaucoma, as an increase in intraocular pressure may occur from the anticholinergic actions of the medication. Older adults are more susceptible to the anticholinergic effects of chlorpheniramine, including possible precipitation of undiagnosed glaucoma.
    Headline
    seizures
    General Information
    Opioids may increase seizure frequency in individuals with seizure disorders and may increase seizure risk in other clinical settings associated with seizures. Regularly evaluate individuals with a history of seizure disorders for worsened seizure control during opioid therapy.
    Headline
    adolescents, children, infants, major surgery, neonates, neuromuscular disease, obesity, obstructive sleep apnea, pulmonary disease
    General Information
    The Key Potentially Inappropriate Drugs in Pediatrics (KIDs) list includes codeine as a potentially inappropriate medication for use in neonates, infants, children, and adolescents and use should be avoided. If avoidance is not possible, use codeine with caution and appropriate monitoring. Chlorpheniramine; codeine is contraindicated in neonates, infants, and children younger than 12 years and in pediatric individuals younger than 18 years after a tonsillectomy and/or adenoidectomy. Guidelines recommend against the use of codeine in all pediatric individuals for any indication. Avoid codeine use in children and adolescents 12 to 18 years of age who have other risk factors for respiratory depression unless the benefits outweigh the risks. Risk factors include conditions associated with hypoventilation such as recent major surgery, obstructive sleep apnea, obesity, severe pulmonary disease, neuromuscular disease, and concomitant use of other medications that can cause respiratory depression. When prescribing codeine for adolescents, choose the lowest effective dose for the shortest period of time and inform individuals and caregivers of the risks and the signs of opioid overdose.  Codeine metabolism is highly variable and unpredictable, particularly in children younger than 12 years; therapeutic response to recommended doses can range from lack of effect in poor metabolizers to fatality in ultrarapid metabolizers. Ultrarapid metabolizers are more likely to convert codeine to morphine quickly, leading to excessive morphine blood concentrations that can result in fatal respiratory depression. Because some children who are normal metabolizers can convert codeine to morphine at rates similar to ultra-rapid metabolizers, this concern extends to all pediatric individuals.
    Headline
    geriatric
    General Information
    Geriatric adults may have increased sensitivity to opioids. Use cautious dose selection for opioids in geriatric adults, usually starting at the low end of the dosing range, reflecting the greater frequency of decreased hepatic, renal, or cardiac function and concomitant disease or other medication therapy. Increase the opioid dosage slowly in geriatric adults and frequently reevaluate for signs of CNS and respiratory depression. According to the Beers Criteria, opioid agonists are considered potentially inappropriate medications in geriatric adults with a history of falls or fractures and should be avoided in these populations, except in the setting of severe acute pain, since opioids can produce ataxia, impaired psychomotor function, syncope, and additional falls. If an opioid must be used, consider reducing the use of other CNS-active medications that increase the risk of falls and fractures and implement strategies to reduce fall risk. In individuals receiving palliative care, the balance of benefits and harms of medication management may differ from those of the general population of older adults. Additionally, the Beers Criteria identifies first-generation antihistamines as potentially inappropriate medications (PIMs) for geriatric adults, recommending their avoidance due to their high anticholinergic properties, decreased clearance in older age, the development of tolerance when used as sleep aids, and an increased risk of anticholinergic effects and toxicity compared to younger individuals. These medications should particularly be avoided in patients with dementia or cognitive impairment (due to adverse CNS effects), those at high risk for delirium (which can worsen or trigger new-onset delirium), and men with lower urinary tract symptoms or benign prostatic hyperplasia (due to risks of urinary retention or hesitancy).
    Headline
    serious adverse effects
    General Information
    Opioids are associated with serious adverse effects, which may be life-threatening, including addiction, abuse, and misuse, respiratory depression, and neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome (NOWS). Accidental ingestion and concomitant use of certain other medications as well as ultra-rapid metabolism of codeine and other risk factors for life-threatening respiratory depression in children may increase the risk of these serious adverse effects.
    Headline
    labor, obstetric delivery, pregnancy
    General Information
    Available data on chlorpheniramine; codeine use during pregnancy are insufficient to define the risk of major birth defects or miscarriage. Use during pregnancy is not recommended. Opioids cross the placenta. Chlorpheniramine and its metabolites likely cross the placenta. Data from case-control and observational studies evaluating codeine use during human pregnancy and the risk of congenital malformations report inconsistent findings. Some studies of codeine exposure showed an increased risk of overall congenital malformations, while others did not; conclusions are limited due to small sample sizes and important methodologic limitations. The majority of studies examining the use of chlorpheniramine in human pregnancy did not find an association with an increased risk of congenital anomalies. In the few studies reporting an association, no consistent pattern of malformations was noted. Opioids can prolong labor and obstetric delivery by temporarily reducing the strength, duration, and frequency of uterine contractions. This effect is not consistent and may be offset by an increased rate of cervical dilatation, which may shorten labor. In addition, neonates exposed to opioids in utero may present with respiratory depression at birth. Because of this, an opioid antagonist (e.g., naloxone) must be available for reversal of opioid-induced respiratory depression in the neonate at delivery.
    Headline
    people who can cause pregnancy in others, people who may become pregnant, reproductive risk
    General Information
    Counsel people who may become pregnant and people who can cause pregnancy in others about the reproductive risk associated with opioids. Long-term use of opioids may influence the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis, leading to hormonal changes that may manifest as a libido decrease, impotence, erectile dysfunction, amenorrhea, or infertility. The causal role of opioids in the clinical manifestations of hypogonadism is unknown. It is not known whether the effects on fertility are reversible.
    Headline
    neonates and infants exposed to this medication in utero
    General Information
    Neonates and infants exposed to this medication in utero may present with respiratory depression and psycho-physiologic effects at birth. Monitor exposed newborns for signs of excess sedation and respiratory depression. If necessary, administer an opioid antagonist (e.g., naloxone) to reverse the effects of opioid-induced respiratory depression. Long-term use of opioids during pregnancy can result in physical dependence in the exposed infant. Monitor newborns for symptoms of withdrawal and manage accordingly. Neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome may present as irritability, hyperactivity, abnormal sleep pattern, high-pitched cry, tremor, vomiting, diarrhea, and failure to gain weight. It can be life-threatening if not recognized and treated. Onset, duration, and severity of opioid withdrawal may vary based on the specific opioid used, duration of use, timing and amount of last maternal use, and rate of elimination by the infant.
    Headline
    breast-feeding
    General Information
    Advise patients to avoid breast-feeding while taking chlorpheniramine; codeine due to the potential for serious adverse reactions in the breastfed child, including excess sedation and respiratory depression. Codeine and its active metabolite, morphine, are present in human milk. In people with normal CYP2D6 activity, the amount of codeine present in milk is low and dose-dependent. However, CYP2D6 ultrarapid metabolizers convert codeine to morphine more extensively, which may result in elevated morphine concentrations in milk and clinically significant opioid exposure in the breastfed child. Chlorpheniramine is also present in human milk. Although adverse effects in breastfed children have not been reported, chlorpheniramine may reduce milk production because of its anticholinergic effects.

Pregnancy And Lactation
  Breastfeeding
    General Information
    Advise patients to avoid breast-feeding while taking chlorpheniramine; codeine due to the potential for serious adverse reactions in the breastfed child, including excess sedation and respiratory depression. Codeine and its active metabolite, morphine, are present in human milk. In people with normal CYP2D6 activity, the amount of codeine present in milk is low and dose-dependent. However, CYP2D6 ultrarapid metabolizers convert codeine to morphine more extensively, which may result in elevated morphine concentrations in milk and clinically significant opioid exposure in the breastfed child. Chlorpheniramine is also present in human milk. Although adverse effects in breastfed children have not been reported, chlorpheniramine may reduce milk production because of its anticholinergic effects.

Interactions
Abiraterone: (Moderate) Concomitant use of codeine with abiraterone may increase codeine plasma concentrations, but decrease the plasma concentration of the active metabolite, morphine, resulting in reduced efficacy or symptoms of opioid withdrawal. It is recommended to avoid this combination when codeine is being used for cough. If coadministration is necessary, monitor patients closely at frequent intervals and consider a dosage increase of codeine until stable drug effects are achieved. Discontinuation of abiraterone could decrease codeine plasma concentrations and increase morphine plasma concentrations resulting in prolonged opioid adverse reactions, including hypotension, respiratory depression, profound sedation, coma, and death. If abiraterone is discontinued, monitor the patient carefully and consider reducing the opioid dosage if appropriate. Codeine is primarily metabolized by CYP2D6 to morphine, and by CYP3A4 to norcodeine; norcodeine does not have analgesic properties. Abiraterone is a moderate inhibitor of CYP2D6.
Acetaminophen; Aspirin; diphenhydrAMINE: (Major) Reserve concomitant use of opioids and diphenhydramine for patients in whom alternate treatment options are inadequate. Limit dosages and durations to the minimum required and monitor patients closely for respiratory depression and sedation. If concomitant use is necessary, consider prescribing naloxone for the emergency treatment of opioid overdose and monitor for signs of urinary retention or reduced gastric motility. Concomitant use can increase the risk of hypotension, respiratory depression, profound sedation, coma, and death as well as urinary retention and/or severe constipation, which may lead to paralytic ileus.
Acetaminophen; Caffeine; Dihydrocodeine: (Moderate) Concomitant use of opioid agonists with chlorpheniramine may cause excessive sedation and somnolence. Limit the use of opioid pain medication with chlorpheniramine to only patients for whom alternative treatment options are inadequate. If concurrent use is necessary, use the lowest effective doses and minimum treatment durations needed to achieve the desired clinical effect.
Acetaminophen; Caffeine; Pyrilamine: (Moderate) Concomitant use of opioid agonists with pyrilamine may cause excessive sedation and somnolence. Limit the use of opioid pain medication with pyrilamine to only patients for whom alternative treatment options are inadequate. If concurrent use is necessary, use the lowest effective doses and minimum treatment durations needed to achieve the desired clinical effect.
Acetaminophen; Chlorpheniramine; Dextromethorphan: (Moderate) Because of the potential risk and severity of serotonin syndrome, caution should be observed when administering codeine with dextromethorphan. Inform patients taking this combination of the possible increased risk and monitor for the emergence of serotonin syndrome particularly during treatment initiation and dose adjustment. Discontinue all serotonergic agents and initiate symptomatic treatment if serotonin syndrome occurs.
Acetaminophen; Chlorpheniramine; Dextromethorphan; Phenylephrine: (Moderate) Because of the potential risk and severity of serotonin syndrome, caution should be observed when administering codeine with dextromethorphan. Inform patients taking this combination of the possible increased risk and monitor for the emergence of serotonin syndrome particularly during treatment initiation and dose adjustment. Discontinue all serotonergic agents and initiate symptomatic treatment if serotonin syndrome occurs.
Acetaminophen; Chlorpheniramine; Dextromethorphan; Pseudoephedrine: (Moderate) Because of the potential risk and severity of serotonin syndrome, caution should be observed when administering codeine with dextromethorphan. Inform patients taking this combination of the possible increased risk and monitor for the emergence of serotonin syndrome particularly during treatment initiation and dose adjustment. Discontinue all serotonergic agents and initiate symptomatic treatment if serotonin syndrome occurs.

Limit the use of opioid pain medication with chlorpheniramine to only patients for whom alternative treatment options are inadequate. If concurrent use is necessary, use the lowest effective doses and minimum treatment durations needed to achieve the desired clinical effect.

Concomitant use can increase the risk of hypotension, respiratory depression, profound sedation, coma, and death as well as urinary retention and/or severe constipation, which may lead to paralytic ileus. Inform patients taking this combination of the possible increased risk and monitor for the emergence of serotonin syndrome particularly during treatment initiation and dose adjustment. Discontinue all serotonergic agents and initiate symptomatic treatment if serotonin syndrome occurs.

Inform patients taking this combination of the possible increased risk and monitor for the emergence of serotonin syndrome particularly during treatment initiation and dose adjustment. Discontinue all serotonergic agents and initiate symptomatic treatment if serotonin syndrome occurs.

Discontinue all serotonergic agents and initiate symptomatic treatment if serotonin syndrome occurs.
